A cell
c
of
\square^{P}
can be regarded as a temporary snapshot of an
execution of events in
P
: for
p\in P
,
c(p)
is
0
if
p
has not
yet started,
if
p
is currently active and
1
if
p
has
terminated. This clearly enforces condition
(
5
).

See
[
FJSZ21
, Example 61]
for the construction of a track object
of a particular ipomset.

{lemC}

[
[
FJSZ21
, Proposition 92]
]

\Lang(\square^{P})=\{P\}{\downarrow}
.

The following proposition relates paths with track objects.

Proposition 24
.

Let
X
be a precubical set,
x
and
y
cells of
X
and
P

an ipomset. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a path
\alpha\in\Path_{X}(x,y)
such that
\ev(\alpha)=P
.

(2)

There exists a pc-map
f:\square^{P}\to X

such that
f(c_{\bot}^{P})=x
and
f(c^{\top}_{P})=y
.

Thus, for each HDA
X
,

\Lang(X)=\{P\in\iiPoms\mid\text{{{HDA}}}(\square^{P},X)\neq\emptyset\}.

Proof 8.1
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1) There exists a path

\beta\in\Path_{\square^{P}}(c_{\bot}^{P},c^{\top}_{P})
such that
\ev(\beta)=P

(see
[
FJSZ21
, Proposition 67]
for further information). So

\alpha=f(\beta)
satisfies the conditions required.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2) By induction on the length
n
of
\alpha
.
First, suppose
n=0,1
. We abbreviate
U=\ev(x)
. If
\alpha=(x)
,
then
\square^{P}=\square^{U}
,
c_{\bot}^{P}=c^{\top}_{P}=[\emptyset|U|\emptyset]
and
then
f=\iota_{x}
satisfies (b). If
\alpha=(x\mathrel{\searrow_{B}}\delta^{1}_{B}(x))

is a down-step, then
P={}_{U}{U}_{U\setminus B}
,
\square^{P}=\square^{U}
,

c_{\bot}^{P}=[\emptyset|U|\emptyset]
,
c^{\top}_{P}=[\emptyset|U|B]
, and,
as before,
f=\iota_{x}
satisfies (b). The proof for up-steps is
symmetric.

If
n>1
, then
\alpha=\beta*\gamma
for paths
\beta
,
\gamma
of
length
<n
. We write
z=\mathsf{tgt}(\beta)=\mathsf{src}(\gamma)
,
Q=\ev(\beta)
,

R=\ev(\gamma)
for short. By the inductive hypothesis, there are
precubical maps
g:\square^{Q}\to X
and
h:\square^{R}\to X
such that

g(c_{\bot}^{Q})=x
,
g(c^{\top}_{Q})=h(c_{\bot}^{R})=z
and
h(c^{\top}_{R})=y
.
Let
U=\ev(z)\cong T_{Q}\cong S_{R}
. By
[
FJSZ21
, Lemma 65]
,
there is a pushout diagram

\square^{Q}
\square^{P}
\square^{U}
\square^{R}
\iota_{c^{\top}_{Q}}
j_{Q}
\iota_{c_{\bot}^{R}}
j_{R}

such that

j_{Q}(c_{\bot}^{Q})=c_{\bot}^{P}
and
j_{R}(c^{\top}_{R})=c^{\top}_{P}
. Since

g\circ\iota_{c^{\top}_{Q}}=\iota_{z}=h\circ\iota_{c_{\bot}^{R}}
, by the
universal property of pushouts, the maps
g
and
h
glue to a map

f:\square^{P}\to X
. Moreover, we have

f(c_{\bot}^{P})=f(j_{Q}(c_{\bot}^{P}))=g(c_{\bot}^{Q})=x
and

f(c^{\top}_{P})=f(j_{R}(c^{\top}_{R}))=h(c^{\top}_{R})=y
.

Proposition 25
.

Languages of HDAs are down-closed with respect to subsumption.

Proof 8.2
.

Let
X
be an HDA. If
P\sqsubseteq Q
and
Q\in\Lang(X)
then there is
an HDA-map
\square^{Q}\to X
by Proposition
24
and

P\in\Lang(\square^{Q})
by Lemma
8
. Thus

P\in\Lang(X)
by Lemma
13
.

9.
Tensor product of higher-dimensional automata

The
tensor product
of HDAs
X
and
Y
is the HDA
X\otimes Y

defined, for
U,V,W\in\square
,
x\in X[V]
,
y\in Y[W]
and
A,B\subseteq U
as

\displaystyle(X\otimes Y)[U]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{V\parallel W=U}X[V]\times Y[W],

\displaystyle\delta_{A,B}(x,y)

\displaystyle=(\delta_{A\cap V,B\cap V}(x),\delta_{A\cap W,B\cap W}(y)),

\displaystyle(X\otimes Y)_{\bot}

\displaystyle=X_{\bot}\times Y_{\bot},

\displaystyle(X\otimes Y)^{\top}

\displaystyle=X^{\top}\times Y^{\top}.

See
[
FJSZ21
, Example 107]
for an example. The following
proposition is shown for event-consistent HDAs in
[
FJSZ21
, Theorem
108]
. We need a proof without this restriction.

Proposition 26
.

Let
X
and
Y
be HDAs. Then

\Lang(X\otimes Y)=\Lang(X)\parallel\Lang(Y)
.

Proof 9.1
.

For
\Lang(X)\parallel\Lang(Y)\subseteq\Lang(X\otimes Y)
the
argument in
[
FJSZ21
, Theorem 108]
works: if

P\in\Lang(X)\parallel\Lang(Y)
, then, by definition, there are

Q\in\Lang(X)
and
R\in\Lang(Y)
such that
P\sqsubseteq Q\parallel R
.
By Proposition
24
, there are HDA-maps

\boldsymbol{\alpha}:\square^{Q}\to X
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}:\square^{R}\to Y
.
Their composition

\square^{P}\to\square^{Q||R}\cong\square^{Q}\otimes\square^{R}\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\otimes\boldsymbol{\beta}}X\otimes Y

shows that
P\in\Lang(X\otimes Y)
. Finally, the isomorphism

\square^{Q||R}\cong\square^{Q}\otimes\square^{R}
is shown in
[
FJSZ21
, Lemma
105]
.

The proof of the converse direction in
[
FJSZ21
]
depends on
event consistency, so we need another one. Suppose

\alpha=((x_{0},y_{0}),\varphi_{1},\dots,(x_{n},y_{n}))\in\Path_{X\otimes Y}
,
and
x_{k}\in X[U_{k}]
as well as
y_{k}\in Y[V_{k}]
, for
k=0,\dots,n
.
For any
k
,

•

if

\varphi_{k}=d^{0}_{A}\in\square(U_{k-1}\parallel V_{k-1},U_{k}\parallel V_{k})
, then we put
\psi_{k}=d^{0}_{A\cap U_{k}}\in\square(U_{k-1},U_{k})
and

\omega_{k}=d^{0}_{A\cap V_{k}}\in\square(V_{k-1},V_{k})
.

•

If
\varphi_{k}=d^{1}_{B}
, we put
\psi_{k}=d^{1}_{B\cap U_{k-1}}
and

\omega_{k}=d^{1}_{B\cap V_{k-1}}
.

It is then routine to check that

\beta=(x_{0},\psi_{0},\dots,x_{n})\in\Path_{X}
and

\gamma=(y_{0},\omega_{0},\dots,y_{n})\in\Path_{Y}
. We write

\pi_{X}(\alpha)=\beta
,
\pi_{Y}(\alpha)=\gamma
.

We prove that
\ev(\alpha)\sqsubseteq\ev(\beta)\parallel\ev(\gamma)
by
induction on
n
.
If
n=1
and
\alpha
is an up-step, then

\ev(\alpha)={}_{(U_{0}\parallel V_{0})}{(U_{1}\parallel V_{1})}_{(U_{1}\parallel V_{1})}={}_{U_{0}}{(U_{1})}_{U_{1}}\parallel{}_{V_{0}}{(V_{1})}_{V_{1}}=\ev(\beta)\parallel\ev(\gamma).

For down-step the same formula holds by symmetry.
The case
n=0
is similar.

If
n>1
, then
\alpha
can be decomposed into a non-trivial
composition
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}*\alpha^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\beta^{\prime}=\pi_{X}(\alpha^{\prime})
,

\gamma^{\prime}=\pi_{Y}(\alpha^{\prime})
,
\beta^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{X}(\alpha^{\prime\prime})
,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\pi_{Y}(\alpha^{\prime\prime})
. Using the inductive hypothesis and the
weak interchange law

(P\parallel P^{\prime})*(Q\parallel Q^{\prime})\sqsubseteq(P*Q)\parallel(P^{\prime}*Q^{\prime})
of
ipomsets
[
FJSZ22b
]
,

\displaystyle\ev(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\ev(\alpha^{\prime})*\ev(\alpha^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq(\ev(\beta^{\prime})\parallel\ev(\beta^{\prime\prime}))*(\ev(\gamma^{\prime})\parallel\ev(\gamma^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq(\ev(\beta^{\prime})*\ev(\beta^{\prime\prime}))\parallel(\ev(\gamma^{\prime})*\ev(\gamma^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle=\ev(\beta^{\prime}*\beta^{\prime\prime})\parallel\ev(\gamma^{\prime}*\gamma^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\ev(\beta)\parallel\ev(\gamma).

Now let
P\in\Lang(X\otimes Y)
and let

\alpha\in\Path_{X\otimes Y}
be an accepting path such that

\ev(\alpha)
. Then both
\beta=\pi_{X}(\alpha)\in\Path_{X}
and

\gamma=\pi_{Y}(\alpha)\in\Path_{Y}
are accepting, and

P=\ev(\alpha)\sqsubseteq\ev(\beta)\parallel\ev(\gamma).

As
\ev(\alpha)\in\Lang(X)
and
\ev(\beta)\in\Lang(Y)
,
down-closure yields
P\in\Lang(X)\parallel\Lang(Y)
.

It follows that parallel compositions of regular languages are
regular.

Proof 9.2

(Proof of Proposition
18
)
.

Let
L
and
M
be regular languages. Then
L=\Lang(X)
and

M=\Lang(Y)
for some HDA
X
and
Y
. Thus
L\parallel M=\Lang(X)\parallel\Lang(Y)=\Lang(X\otimes Y)
is recognised by

X\otimes Y
by Proposition
26
and therefore
regular.

10.
Higher-dimensional automata with interfaces

In this section we introduce higher-dimensional automata with
interfaces (iHDAs). The main difference to HDAs is that the elements
in the lists of concurrent events that are assigned to cells are now
equipped with interfaces. Every event of an iHDA can thus be labelled
as a source event or a target event, or as both. Target events may
not be terminated while source events cannot be “unstarted”.
Moreover, start cells must only contain source events, and target
cells only target events. The advantage of this variant is that
source and target events of an iHDA can be traced along its execution,
as shown for instance in Figure
16
below.

Concurrency lists with interfaces

A
concurrency list with interfaces

(
iconclist
) is a triple
(S,U,T)
of a conclist
U
, a
source
interface

S\subseteq U
and a
target interface

T\subseteq U
.

We also write
{}_{S}{U}_{T}
or just
U
for an iconclist
(S,U,T)
.
In the latter case, we write
S_{U}
and
T_{U}
for the interfaces of

U
. Conclists may be regarded as iconclists with empty interfaces and
iconclists as discrete ipomsets.

An
ilo-map

f:U\to V
is an lo-map that also
satisfies
S_{U}=f^{-1}(S_{V})
and
T_{U}=f^{-1}(T_{V})
.
An
iconclist isomorphism
is an invertible ilo-map. We write
U\cong V

if iconclists
U
and
V
are isomorphic.

As for conclists, there is at most one isomorphism between iconclists.
Isomorphism classes of iconclists can be modelled as words over the
extended alphabet

\Sigma_{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}=\{a,\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a,a\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}},\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\mid a\in\Sigma\}
, where
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a
indicates membership in a source
interface and so on. As in Figure
1
, we represent
such words as column vectors.

Let
U=(S_{U},U,T_{U})
and
V=(S_{V},V,T_{V})
be iconclists.
An
iconclist map
from
U
to
V

is a conclist map
d_{A,B}:U\to V
such that

•

source and target events are preserved:
d_{A,B}^{-1}(S_{V})=S_{U}

and
d_{A,B}^{-1}(T_{V})=T_{U}
,

•

source events cannot be unstarted and target events not be
terminated:
A\cap S_{V}=\emptyset=B\cap T_{V}
.

See Figure
15
for an example.
Compositions of iconclist maps are defined as for conclist maps.

\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}b\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}
\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}
0
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}b\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}e\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}
1

Figure 15.
An example of an iconclist map.
Annotations
0
and
1

indicate events that have not yet started (
0
) or
terminated (
1
), as in Figure
7
.
Bullets indicate source and target interfaces.
Note that
c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}
cannot be marked by
1
since it is in the target interface; similarly,
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}e
cannot be marked by
0
.
No marking is possible for
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}b\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}
and thus it must be in the image.

The
full labelled precube category with interfaces
,
I
,
has iconclists as objects and iconclist maps as morphisms.
Every pair of isomorphic iconclists admits exactly one isomorphism
between them.
Thus, we define the
labelled precube category with interfaces

{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
as the
quotient of
I
by isomorphisms.
The quotient functor
{\textup{I}}\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\to{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square

is an equivalence of categories.
The category
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
is skeletal, and its objects are words on

\Sigma_{\bullet}=\{a,\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ {a},a\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ ,\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ a\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ \mid a\in\Sigma\}
.

We can assign an iconclist with empty interfaces to any conclist using
the inclusion functors

\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ni U\mapsto{}_{\emptyset}{U}_{\emptyset}\in{\textup{I}}\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and

\square\to{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
. Conversely, there are forgetful functors
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\to\square

and
{\textup{I}}\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\to\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
that ignore interfaces and assign the
underlying conclist to each iconclist.

The involutive
reversal
functor on
I
and
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
maps

{}_{S}{U}_{T}
to
{}_{T}{U}_{S}
and
d_{A,B}
to
d_{B,A}
. It
swaps events that have not yet started and those that have terminated.

Precubical sets with interfaces and iHDAs

A
precubical set with interfaces
(
ipc-set
) is a presheaf
on
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
. We write
X[U]
for the value of
X
on object
U
of

{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
, and
\delta_{A,B}=X[d_{A,B}]:X[U]\to X[U\setminus(A\cup B)]

for the
face map
associated to the coface map

d_{A,B}:U\setminus(A\cup B)\to U
. Elements of
X[U]
are

cells
of
X
. We write
\Cell(X)=\bigsqcup_{U\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square}X[U]
for
the set of cells of
X
. An ipc-set
Y
is an
ipc-subset
of

X
if
Y[U]\subseteq X[U]
for all
U\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
and the face maps of

Y
are the restrictions of face maps of
X
.

If
x\in X[{}_{S}{U}_{T}]
, we write
\Iev(x)={}_{S}{U}_{T}\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
and

\ev(x)=U\in\square
. As before, we also write
\delta_{A}^{0}
for

X[d_{A}^{0}]
and
\delta_{B}^{1}
for
X[d_{B}^{1}]
. We may view a precubical
set as an ipc-set
X
such that
X[{}_{S}{U}_{T}]=\emptyset
whenever

S\neq\emptyset
or
T\neq\emptyset
.
As for pc-sets,

\Cell(X)
may be regarded as the category of elements of
X
,
with the projection functor
\Iev:\Cell(X)\to{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
.
We often view
X
as a set of cells, that is, objects of
\Cell(X)
.

A
higher-dimensional automaton with interfaces
(
iHDA
)
is a finite ipc-set
X
with subsets
X_{\bot}
of
start cells
and

X^{\top}
of
accept cells
.
These are required to satisfy
S=U
for all
x\in X_{\bot}
with
\Iev(x)={}_{S}{U}_{T}
,
and
T=U
for all
x\in X^{\top}
with
\Iev(x)={}_{S}{U}_{T}
.
Neither
X_{\bot}
nor
X^{\top}
is necessarily an ipc-subset.

HDAs are not simply special cases of iHDAs
due to the above requirements on interfaces of start and accept cells.
See Figure
16
for examples.

An
ipc-map
is a natural transformation
f:X\to Y
of ipc-sets

X
,
Y
, an
iHDA-map
must preserve start and accept cells as
well:
f(X_{\bot})\subseteq Y_{\bot}
and
f(X^{\top})\subseteq Y^{\top}
. We
write
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\text{{{Set}}}
and
iHDA
for the resulting categories of ipc-sets
and iHDAs.

The reversal on
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
translates to ipc-sets and iHDAs. It maps

\delta_{A,B}
to
\delta_{B,A}
and exchanges start and accept cells
if present.

Standard icubes

[b|U|a]
[b|U|\emptyset]
[\emptyset|U|a]
[\emptyset|U|\emptyset]
[\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a]
[\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a]
[b]
[c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
[c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
[d\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\\
\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}b\end{smallmatrix}\right]
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}b\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}\\
c\bullet\end{smallmatrix}\right]
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\\
d\bullet\end{smallmatrix}\right]
\bot
\top

Figure 16.
Left: the standard icube
\square^{U}
for
U={\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\,a\,\phantom{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\\
\phantom{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\,b\,\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ \end{smallmatrix}\right]}
with names of cells.
Right: an example of an iHDA with iconclists associated to
particular cells.
The presence of interfaces causes that some faces are “missing”.
Those are indicated by dashed lines or circles.

The
standard icube

{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
is the presheaf represented by an iconclist
U\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
,
that is,
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}[W]={\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square(W,U)
.
Every morphism
d_{A,B}\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square(U,V)

defines an ipc-map

{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}}:{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}\to{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{V},

which gives a functor
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\ni U\mapsto{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\text{{{Set}}}
.

An example of a standard icube is given on the left in
Figure
16
.

The analogue of Lemma
3
holds for iHDA as well.
For every iHDA
X
and cell
x\in X[U]

there exists a unique iHDA-map
\iota_{x}:{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}\to X

such that
\iota_{x}(\id_{U})=x
.
(Note that
\id_{U}=[\emptyset|U|\emptyset]\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square(U,U)\cong{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}[U]
is the top cell of
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
).

Executions of iHDAs

Paths in iHDAs, their ipomsets and languages are defined by analogy to
HDAs in Section
5
. A path in an iHDA
X
is a
sequence
(x_{0},\varphi_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
such that every step

(x_{k-1},\varphi_{k},x_{k})
is either an up-step

\delta^{0}_{A}(x)\mathrel{\nearrow^{A}}x=(\delta^{0}_{A}(x),d^{0}_{A},x)
or a down-step

x\mathrel{\searrow_{B}}\delta^{1}_{B}(x)=(x,d^{B}_{1},\delta^{1}_{B}(x))
. The ipomset of a
path is a gluing composition of ipomsets of consecutive steps, which
are
{}_{(U\setminus A)}{U}_{U}
and
{}_{U}{U}_{(U\setminus B)}
for
up-steps and down-steps as specified above.

The set of ipomsets of accepting paths forms the language
\Lang(X)

of
X
. We will see in the next section that
\Lang(X)
is
down-closed, that is, an interval ipomset language. Further, any iHDA
can be translated to an HDA that recognises the same language, and
vice versa.

As for HDA maps, we call an iHDA map
f:X\to Y
a
weak
equivalence
if for every accepting path
\beta\in\Path_{Y}
there is
an accepting path
\alpha\in\Path_{X}
such that
f(\alpha)=\beta
.
The analogue of Lemma
13
holds for iHDA:
for every iHDA map
f:X\to Y
we have
\Lang(X)\subseteq\Lang(Y)
,
and
\Lang(X)=\Lang(Y)
if
f
is a weak equivalence.

11.
Higher-dimensional automata with and without interfaces

In this section we discuss the relationship between HDAs and iHDAs.
We show that there are two functors that translate between them: the
resolution
\Res:\text{{{HDA}}}\to\text{{{iHDA}}}
and the closure
\Cl:\text{{{iHDA}}}\to\text{{{HDA}}}
,
both of which preserve languages. Finite HDAs and finite iHDAs
therefore recognise the same class of regular languages.

Resolution

The
resolution
of a precubical set
X
is the ipc-set

\Res(X)=X\circ\mathsf{F}
, where
\mathsf{F}:{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\to\square
is the forgetful
functor.

For
{}_{S}{U}_{T}\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
and
A,B\subseteq U
, the definition of

\Res
expands to

\displaystyle\Res(X)[{}_{S}{U}_{T}]

\displaystyle=\{(x;S,T)\mid x\in X[U]\},

\displaystyle\delta_{A,B}((x;S,T))

\displaystyle=(\delta_{A,B}(x);S\setminus B,T\setminus A).

The functor
F:{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\to\square
forgets interfaces, but the composition

{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\to}}\square\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{\to}}\text{{{Set}}}
produces copies of
cells of
X
equipped with all possible combinations of interfaces.
Notation such as
(x;S,T)
indicates that
\Res(X)[{}_{S}{U}_{T}]
is
essentially the same set as
X[U]
, but each
x\in X[U]
is tagged
with
S
and
T
. For every HDA
X
we define

(x;S,T)\in\Res(X)[{}_{S}{U}_{T}]
to be a start cell if
x\in X_{\bot}

and
S=U
, and an accept cell if
x\in X^{\top}
and
T=U
.

This extends
\Res
to a functor
\text{{{HDA}}}\to\text{{{iHDA}}}
. Every cell

x\in X[U]
produces
4^{|U|}
cells in
\Res(X)
. Thus
\Res(X)
is
finite whenever
X
is.

{exa}

For an HDA
X
with
x\in X[a]
and
v,w\in X[\emptyset]
,

\Res\left(\vbox{\hbox{
\hbox to110.78pt{\vbox to44.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.68193pt\lower-5.2705pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Proposition 27
.

If
X
is an HDA, then
\Lang(\Res(X))=\Lang(X)
.

Proof 11.1
.

If

((x_{0};S_{0},T_{0}),\varphi_{1},(x_{1};S_{1},T_{1}),\varphi_{2},\dotsc,\varphi_{n},(x_{n};S_{n},T_{n}))

is an accepting path in
\Res(X)
, then

(x_{0},\varphi_{1},x_{1},\varphi_{2}\dots,\varphi_{n},x_{n})
is an accepting path in

X
with the same event ipomset. Conversely, if

\alpha=(x_{0},\varphi_{1},x_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n},x_{n})
,
x_{k}\in X[U_{k}]
is an
accepting path in
X
, we define
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
recursively (recall
that, by Lemma
10
, the
U_{k}
may be taken as
subsets of
\ev(\alpha)
):

•

S_{0}=U_{0}
,
T_{n}=U_{n}
,

•

if
\varphi_{k}=d^{0}_{U_{k}\setminus U_{k-1}}
, then
S_{k}=S_{k-1}
and

T_{k-1}=T_{k}\cap U_{k-1}
,

•

if
\varphi_{k}=d^{1}_{U_{k-1}\setminus U_{k}}
, then

S_{k}=S_{k-1}\cap U_{k}
and
T_{k-1}=T_{k}
.

This yields an accepting path
((x_{k};S_{k},T_{k}),\varphi_{k})
in

\Res(X)
with the same event ipomset as
\alpha
in
X
.

Closure

The closure is the left adjoint to resolution, though we neither need
nor prove this fact in this article. Instead, we give an explicit
definition.

The
closure
of the ipc-set
X
is the pc-set
\Cl(X)
defined,
for all
U\in\square
, as

\Cl(X)[U]=\{[x;A,B]\mid\exists\,{}_{S}{V}_{T}\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square:x\in X[V],A\subseteq S,B\subseteq T,A\cap B=\emptyset,U=V\setminus(A\cup B)\}.

We write
[x;A,B]
instead of
(x;A,B)
to distinguish the provenance
of these elements. Face maps are given by

\delta_{C,D}([x;A,B])=[\delta_{C\setminus S,D\setminus T}(x);A\cup(C\cap S),B\cup(D\cap T)],

where
U
and
{}_{S}{V}_{T}
are as above and the
C,D\subseteq U

satisfy
C\cap D=\emptyset
. An ipc-map
f:X\to Y
induces a pc-map

\Cl(f):\Cl(X)\to\Cl(Y)
such that
\Cl(f)[U]([x;A,B])=[f(x);A,B]
.
This makes
\Cl:{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\text{{{Set}}}\to\square\text{{{Set}}}
a functor.

Intuitively,
\Cl(X)
fills in the missing cells of the ipc-set
X
.
The function
\delta_{C\setminus S_{V},D\setminus T_{V}}
takes as much of
the face map as possible, while the remaining events that should be
unstarted or terminated are added to
A
and
B
, respectively. See
Figure
17
for an example.

For an iHDA
X
we define

\Cl(X)_{\bot}=\{[x;\emptyset,\emptyset]\mid x\in X_{\bot}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Cl(X)^{\top}=\{[x;\emptyset,\emptyset]\mid x\in X^{\top}\}.

This extends
\Cl
to a functor
\text{{{iHDA}}}\to\text{{{HDA}}}
, which is the left
adjoint of the extension of the functor
\Res
.

a
b
c
c^{\prime}
\top
d
x
y
z
q
\bot
[x;a,\emptyset]
[x;b,\emptyset]
[a;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[y;b,\emptyset]
[c;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[c^{\prime};\emptyset,\emptyset]
\top
[y;\emptyset,c]
[z;\emptyset,c]
[x;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[y;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[z;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[q;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[a;a,\emptyset]
[x;ab,\emptyset]
[y;b,c]
[c;\emptyset,c]
[c^{\prime};\emptyset,c]
\bot

Figure 17.
An example of an iHDA (left) and its closure (right).
Not all cells are captioned.
The iconclists of cells on the left are

\Iev(x)=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\\
\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}b\end{smallmatrix}\right]
,

\Iev(y)=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}b\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}\\
\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
,

\Iev(z)=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}c\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\\
d\phantom{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.

Lemma 28
.

If
{}_{S}{U}_{T}
is an iconclist, then

\Cl({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{{}_{S}{U}_{T}})\cong\square^{U}
.

Proof 11.2
.

The isomorphism maps a cell
[A|B]\in\square^{U}[U\setminus(A\cup B)]
into

[[(A\setminus S)|(B\setminus T)];A\cap S,B\cap T]

in the set
\Cl({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{{}_{S}{U}_{T}})[U\setminus(A\cup B)]
.

Proposition 29
.

If
X
is an iHDA, then
\Lang(\Cl(X))=\Lang(X)
.

Proof 11.3
.

If
(x_{0},\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{n},x_{n})
is an accepting path in
X
,
then

([x_{0};\emptyset,\emptyset],\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{n},[x_{n};\emptyset,\emptyset])

is an accepting path in
\Cl(X)
. Conversely,
let
\alpha=([x_{0};A_{0},B_{0}],\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{n},[x_{n};A_{n},B_{n}])
be
a path in
\Cl(X)
. Then, for all
\varphi_{k}=d^{0}_{C_{k}}
, we have

A_{k-1}\supseteq A_{k}
and
B_{k-1}=B_{k}
, and for all

\varphi_{k}=d^{1}_{D_{k}}
,
A_{k-1}=A_{k}
and
B_{k-1}\subseteq B_{k}
.
Hence
A_{0}\supseteq\dotsm\supseteq A_{n}
and

B_{0}\subseteq\dotsm\subseteq B_{n}
. If
\alpha
is accepting, then

A_{0}=B_{n}=\emptyset
and thus
A_{k}=B_{k}=\emptyset
for all
k
.

Proposition 30
.

HDAs and iHDAs recognise the same class of languages: that of
regular languages.

Proof 11.4
.

Resolution and closure preserves finiteness of automata. The result
then follows from Propositions
27

and
29
.

We conclude with an easy technical lemma that is needed later on.

Lemma 31
.

Let
X
be an iHDA,
x\in X
and
[y;A,B]\in\Cl(X)
. Then

[y;A,B]
is a face of
[x;\emptyset,\emptyset]
in
\Cl(X)
if and
only if
y
is a face of
x
in
X
.

Proof 11.5
.

If
y=\delta_{C,D}(x)
, then

\delta_{A,B}(\delta_{C,D}([x;\emptyset,\emptyset]))=\delta_{A,B}([y;\emptyset,\emptyset])=[y;A,B],

and if
[y;A,B]=\delta_{C,D}([x;\emptyset,\emptyset])
, then

y=\delta_{C\setminus S,D\setminus T}(x)
, where

S=S_{\Iev(x)}
,
T=T_{\Iev(x)}
.

Simple languages

An iHDA
X
is

start simple
if it has exactly one start cell,

accept simple
if it has exactly one accept cell,
and
simple
if it is both start and accept simple.
A regular language is
simple
if it is
recognised by a simple iHDA.

{exa}

HDAs with one start and one accept cell recognise a larger class of
languages that simple iHDA. The HDA
X
with a single
0
-cell
x
,
a
1
-loop labelled
a
on
x
, and
X_{\bot}=X^{\top}=\{a\}
is simple
and recognises the language of all ipomsets

[\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\dotsm a\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
, but no simple iHDA does. This is
because
[\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
may only be an ipomset of a constant
path
(x)
such that
x\in X[\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}a\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
and

x\in X_{\bot}\cap X^{\top}
. Yet the event
a
cannot be terminated in
any path starting at
x
, so no such path may recognise

[\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}aa\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}]
.

Lemma 32
.

Every regular language is a finite union of simple regular languages.

Proof 11.6
.

Proposition
30
allows us to work with a suitable iHDA

X
. Let
X_{\bot}=\{x_{\bot}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and

X^{\top}=\{x^{\top}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
. For each pair

(i,j)
, let
X_{i}^{j}
be the iHDA with the same underlying ipc-set as

X
and
(X_{i}^{j})_{\bot}=\{x_{\bot}^{i}\}
,
(X_{i}^{j})^{\top}=\{x^{\top}_{j}\}
.
Then
\Lang(X)=\bigcup_{i,j}\Lang(X_{i}^{j})
.

Intuitively, we switch off all start and accept states but one of each
in this proof.

Normal form of paths on closures of iHDAs

The next lemma gives a normal form for paths on HDAs that are closures
of iHDAs.

Lemma 33
.

Let
X\in\text{{{iHDA}}}
,
\alpha\in\Path_{\Cl(X)}
. Then
\alpha
is
subsumed by a path of the form

([x;C\cup A,D]\mathrel{\nearrow^{A}}[x;C,D])*\beta*([y;C,D]\mathrel{\searrow_{B}}[y;C,D\cup B]),

where
\beta=([x_{0};C,D],\varphi_{0},\dots,[x_{n};C,D])
.

See Figure
18
for an example.
Note that the restriction of
\alpha=([x_{k};A_{k},B_{k}],\varphi_{k})\in\Path_{\Cl(X)}

to the first coordinate gives a path
\alpha^{\prime}=(x_{k},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})\in\Path_{X}

with, possibly, trivial steps (
i.e.
,
\varphi_{k}^{\prime}=d^{0}_{\emptyset}
or
\varphi_{k}^{\prime}=d^{1}_{\emptyset}
).
In Lemma
33
we regard
A,B,C,D
as subsets of
\ev(\alpha^{\prime})
.

Proof 11.7
.

Without loss of generality we assume that

\alpha
is a
dense
path, that is, all its steps either start (
d^{0}_{\{a\}}
) or terminate (
d^{1}_{\{a\}}
) a single event
a
.
Every single step
\sigma
in
\alpha
falls into one of three categories:

(
+
)
\sigma=[x;A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[x;A,B\cup\{b\}]
for
b\in T_{\ev(x)}
,

(
-
)
\sigma=[x;A\cup\{a\},B]\mathrel{\nearrow^{a}}[x;A,B]
for
a\in S_{\ev(x)}
,

(
0
) neither of the above, so

\sigma=[x;A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[\delta^{1}_{b}(x);A,B]

or
\sigma=[\delta^{0}_{a}(x);A,B]\mathrel{\nearrow^{a}}[x;A,B]
.

We show that the steps of
\alpha
can be rearranged so that all
(
0
)-steps are preceded by (
-
)-steps and succeeded by
(
+
)-steps. Let
{}_{S}{U}_{T}=\Iev(x)
. For
b\in T
,

c\in U\setminus T
,

\left([x;A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[x;A,B\cup\{b\}]\mathrel{\searrow_{c}}[\delta^{1}_{c}(x);A,B\cup\{b\}]\right)\\
\simeq\left([x;A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{c}}[\delta^{1}_{c}(x);A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[\delta^{1}_{c}(x);A,B\cup\{b\}]\right).

For
a\in U\setminus S
,
b\in T
,

\left([\delta^{0}_{a}(x);A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[\delta^{0}_{a}(x);A,B\cup\{b\}]\mathrel{\nearrow^{a}}[x;A,B\cup\{b\}]\right)\\
\sqsubseteq\left([\delta^{0}_{a}(x);A,B]\mathrel{\nearrow^{a}}[x;A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[x;A,B\cup\{b\}]\right).

Thus every (
+
)-step followed by a (
0
)-step, can be swapped,
possibly passing to a subsuming path. Likewise, we can swap every
(
0
)-step followed by a (
-
)-step. Further, for
a\in S
,
b\in T
,

\left([x;A\cup\{a\},B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[x;A\cup\{a\},B\cup\{b\}]\mathrel{\nearrow^{a}}[x;A,B\cup\{b\}]\right)\\
\sqsubseteq\left([x;A\cup\{a\},B]\mathrel{\nearrow^{a}}[x;A,B]\mathrel{\searrow_{b}}[x;A,B\cup\{b\}]\right),

so every (
+
)-step followed by a (
-
)-step can be swapped, too.
Finally, we can concatenate all (
-
)-steps, (
0
)-steps and (
+
)
steps to obtain the conclusion.

[x;b,\emptyset]
[y;b,\emptyset]
[y;\emptyset,c]
[z;\emptyset,c]
[y;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[x;ab,\emptyset]
[b;b,\emptyset]
[c;\emptyset,c]
[c^{\prime};\emptyset,c]
\alpha
[c^{\prime};\emptyset,\emptyset]
[x;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[y;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[z;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[x;ab,\emptyset]
[c^{\prime};\emptyset,c]
[b;\emptyset,\emptyset]
[c;\emptyset,\emptyset]
\beta

Figure 18.
An illustration of Lemma
33

using the iHDA from Figure
17
.
The orange path
\alpha
on the left
is subsumed by
([x;ab,\emptyset]\mathrel{\nearrow^{ab}}[x;\emptyset,\emptyset])*\beta*([c^{\prime};\emptyset,\emptyset]\mathrel{\searrow_{c}}[c^{\prime};\emptyset,c])
on the right.

12.
Cylinders

In this section we introduce cylinders for ipc-sets. This
construction is motivated by the double mapping cylinder from
topology
and may be regarded as a significant generalization of resolving
\varepsilon
-transitions.
For a pair of ipc-maps
f:Y\to X
and
g:Z\to X
, we
construct an ipc-set
\Cyl(f,g)
, which is equivalent to
X
in a
sense explained below. This allows us to replace
f
and

g
by injections
\tilde{f}:Y\to\Cyl(f,g)
and

\tilde{g}:Z\to\Cyl(f,g)
whose images are initial and final in

\Cyl(f,g)
, respectively, in the sense of the following definition.
Cylinders are used later as tools
that separate initial and accept states in iHDAs, that is, which
replace them by proper ones in such a way that the languages
accepted do not change.

Initial and final inclusions

Let
X
be an ipc-set.
A ipc-subset
Y\subseteq X
is
initial

if it is down-closed with respect to the reachability preorder
\preceq
in
X
.
Equivalently,
Y
is initial in
X

if
\delta^{1}_{B}(x)\in Y
implies
x\in Y
for all
x\in X[U]
and

B\subseteq U\setminus T_{U}
.
(Since
Y
is an ipc-set, the implication
x\in Y\implies\delta^{0}_{A}(x)\in Y
follows.)
By reversal,
Y
is
final
if it
is up-closed with respect to
\preceq
or, equivalently,

\delta^{0}_{A}(x)\in Y
implies
x\in Y
.
An
initial
(
final
)
inclusion
is an injective
ipc-map whose image is an initial (final) ipc-subset.

Lemma 34
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be an initial (final) inclusion of ipc-sets. Then
its closure
\Cl(f):\Cl(Y)\to\Cl(X)
is an initial (final) inclusion
of pc-sets.

Proof 12.1
.

Suppose
f
is an initial inclusion,
x\in X[U]
, and
\delta^{1}_{D}([x;A,B])\in\im(\Cl(f))
. Then

\delta^{1}_{D\setminus T_{U}}(x)\in\im(f)
because

\delta^{1}_{D}([x;A,B])=[\delta^{1}_{D\setminus T_{U}}(x);A,B\cup(D\cap T_{U})]\in\im(\Cl(f)).

Thus
x\in\im(f)
because
f
is initial, and

[x;A,B]\in\im(\Cl(f))
follows.
The proof for final inclusions is similar.

Proper iHDAs

The
start
and
accept maps
of an iHDA
X
are the ipc-maps

\iota_{\bot}^{X}=\bigsqcup_{x\in X_{\bot}}\!\!\iota_{x}:\bigsqcup_{x\in X_{\bot}}\!{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}\to X\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\iota^{\top}_{X}=\bigsqcup_{x\in X^{\top}}\!\!\iota_{x}:\bigsqcup_{x\in X^{\top}}\!{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}\to X.

An iHDA is
start proper
if its start map is an initial
inclusion and
accept proper
if its accept map is a final
inclusion. An iHDA is
proper
if it is start proper, accept
proper and the images of the start map and the accept map are
disjoint.

The following lemma and example explain the structure of start and
accept proper iHDAs.

Lemma 35
.

All start cells of start proper iHDAs are
\preceq
-minimal. All
accept cells of accept proper iHDAs are
\preceq
-maximal.

Proof 12.2
.

Let
x_{\bot}\in X_{\bot}
and
U=\Iev(x_{\bot})
. Then obviously

S_{U}=U
. The top cell
c=[\emptyset|U|\emptyset]
of
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
is
thus
\preceq
-minimal, in particular when regarded as a cell in

\bigsqcup_{x\in X_{\bot}}{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}
. But
x_{\bot}=i_{\bot}^{X}(c)

and initial inclusions preserve
\preceq
-minimal elements. So

x_{\bot}
is
\preceq
-minimal. The claim for accept cells follows
by reversal.

{exa}

The condition of Lemma
35
is not sufficient
for properness. The following diagrams show examples of iHDAs that
are not start proper:

\bot
X
\bot
\bot
Y
v
x
x
Z
q
\bot

Edges marked with
x
have been identified. In the first diagram, the
start map
\iota^{X}_{\bot}
is an inclusion, but not initial. In the
second and third diagram, neither
\iota^{Y}_{\bot}
nor
\iota^{Z}_{\bot}
is
an inclusion:
\iota^{Y}_{\bot}
maps two different vertices to
v
;

\iota^{Z}_{\bot}
maps two different edges of
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(q)}
to
x
.

Lifting properties

An ipc-map
f:Y\to X
has the
future lifting property

(
FLP
) if for every up-step
\alpha=(\delta^{0}_{A}(x)\mathrel{\nearrow^{A}}x)
in

X
and every
y\in Y
such that
f(y)=\delta^{0}_{A}(x)
there is an
up-step
\beta=(y\mathrel{\nearrow^{A}}z)
in
Y
such that
f(\beta)=\alpha
. The

past lifting property
(
PLP
) is defined by reversal.

FLP and PLP are equivalent to the lifting properties in the following
diagrams:

FLP:
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U\setminus A}
Y
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
X
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}}
\iota_{y}
\iota_{x}
f
\iota_{z}
PLP:
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U\setminus B}
Y
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
X
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{1}_{B}}
\iota_{y}
\iota_{x}
f
\iota_{z}

The next lemma states that path lifting properties allow to lift paths along
f
,
given that their source or target cells can be lifted.
It is immediate from the definitions.

Lemma 36
.

An ipc-map
f:Y\to X
has the FLP if and only if for every
\alpha\in\Path_{X}

and
y\in f^{-1}(\mathsf{src}(\alpha))
there exists a path

\beta\in\Path_{Y}
such that
\mathsf{src}(\beta)=y
and
f(\beta)=\alpha
.
An analogous property holds for PLP. ∎

Let
f:Y\to X
be an ipc-map and
S,T\subseteq X
, that is, these are
subsets, but not necessarily ipc-subsets. Then
f
has the

total lifting property
(
TLP
) with respect to
S
and
T

if for every path
\alpha\in\Path_{X}
with
\mathsf{src}(\alpha)\in S
and

\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha)\in T
and every
y\in f^{-1}(\mathsf{src}(\alpha))
and

z\in f^{-1}(\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha))
, there exists a path

\beta\in\Path_{Y}(y,z)
such that
f(\beta)=\alpha
.

Proposition 37
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be an iHDA map such that the functions

Y_{\bot}\to X_{\bot}
and
Y^{\top}\to X^{\top}
induced by
f
are
surjective. Suppose at least one of the following holds:

(1)

f
has the future lifting property and
Y^{\top}=f^{-1}(X^{\top})
,

(2)

f
has the past lifting property and
Y_{\bot}=f^{-1}(X_{\bot})
,

(3)

f
has the total lifting property with respect to

X_{\bot}
and
X^{\top}
.

Then
f
is a weak equivalence.

Proof 12.3
.

For (1), there exists an
y\in Y_{\bot}
such that
f(y)=\mathsf{src}(\alpha)

by assumption, and
\beta\in\Path_{Y}
such that
\mathsf{src}(\beta)=y

and
f(\beta)=\alpha
by Lemma
36
. Moreover,

\mathsf{tgt}(\beta)\in f^{-1}(\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha))\subseteq f^{-1}(X^{\top})=Y^{\top}.

Item (2)
follows from (1) by reversal.

For (3), there are
y\in Y_{\bot}
,
z\in Y^{\top}
such that

f(y)=\mathsf{src}(\alpha)
,
f(z)=\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha)
by assumption. Since

f
has the TLP, there exists a
\beta\in\Path_{Y}(y,z)
such
that
f(\beta)=\alpha
.

Cylinders

Let
X,Y,Z\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square\text{{{Set}}}
and
f:Y\to X
,
g:Z\to X
be
ipc-maps. Assume further that
f
and
g
have disjoint images. This
is not directly used in the construction, but crucial in proofs.

The
cylinder

\Cyl(f,g)
is the ipc-set such that

\Cyl(f,g)[U]
is the set of
(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)
, where

•

x\in X[U]
;

•

K
is an initial ipc-subset of
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
;

•

L
is a final ipc-subset of
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
;

•

\varphi:K\to Y
is an ipc-map such that
f\circ\varphi=\iota_{x}|_{K}
;

•

\psi:L\to Z
is an ipc-map such that
g\circ\psi=\iota_{x}|_{L}
.

For

d_{A,B}\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square(V,U)
and
(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)\in\Cyl(f,g)[U]
, we put

\delta_{A,B}(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)=(\delta_{A,B}(x),({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}})^{-1}(K),({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}})^{-1}(L),\varphi\circ{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}},\psi\circ{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}}).

Equivalently,
\Cyl(f,g)[U]
is the set of commutative diagrams of
solid arrows in Figure
19
and the face map

\delta_{A,B}
composes the diagram with the dashed arrows.
The following is then clear (recall that
f(Y)\cap g(Z)=\emptyset
).

Lemma 38
.

Let
(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)\in\Cyl(f,g)
. Then

K\subseteq(\iota_{x})^{-1}(f(Y))
and

L\subseteq(\iota_{x})^{-1}(g(Z))
. Thus
K\cap L=\emptyset
,

x\in f(Y)
implies
L=\emptyset
, and
x\in g(Z)
implies

K=\emptyset
.

\tilde{f}(Y)
Y
\tilde{f}
\tilde{g}
j
p
f
g
\tilde{g}(Z)
Z
j(X)
X
\Cyl(f,g)
({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}})^{-1}(K)
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{V}
({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}})^{-1}(L)
K
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
L
Y
X
Z
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}}
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}}
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d_{A,B}}
\varphi
\iota_{x}
\psi
f
g

Figure 19.
The cylinder
\Cyl(f,g)
and a diagram defining its cell.

\Cyl(f,g)
is equipped with the ipc-maps shown in Figure
19
.
They are defined by

j(x)=(x,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset),\qquad p(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)=x,

\tilde{f}(y)=(f(y),{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(y)},\emptyset,\iota_{y},\emptyset),\qquad\tilde{g}(z)=(g(z),\emptyset,{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(z)},\emptyset,\iota_{z}).

Intuitively,
\Cyl(f,g)
may be regarded as a result of the following procedure:
in the disjoint union of
Y\sqcup X\sqcup Z
,
add
\varepsilon
-transitions
y\to f(y)
for all cells
y\in Y

and
g(z)\to z
for all
z\in Z
.
Next, resolve all
\varepsilon
-transitions.
(Alas, we know no satisfactory definition of
\varepsilon
-transitions for ipc-sets).
See Figure
20
for an example.

Next we collect some of properties of cylinders.

Lemma 39
.

(1)

p\circ\tilde{f}=f
,
p\circ\tilde{g}=g
, and
p\circ j=\id_{X}
.

(2)

\tilde{f}
is an initial inclusion and

\tilde{f}(Y)=\{(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)\in\Cyl(f,g)\mid K={\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)},L=\emptyset\}.

(3)

\tilde{g}
is a final inclusion and

\tilde{g}(Z)=\{(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)\in\Cyl(f,g)\mid K=\emptyset,L={\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}\}.

(4)

j
is an inclusion and
j(X)=\{(x,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\Cyl(f,g)\}
.

(5)

\tilde{f}(Y)
,
\tilde{g}(Z)
and
j(X)
are pairwise disjoint.

Proof 12.4
.

Item (1) is straightforward from the definition.

For (2), let
Y^{\prime}\subseteq\Cyl(f,g)
be the right-hand side of the
equation. We show that

h:Y^{\prime}\ni(x,{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)},\emptyset,\varphi,\emptyset)\mapsto\varphi([\emptyset|\emptyset])\in Y

is the inverse of
\tilde{f}:Y\to Y^{\prime}
.
Indeed,

h(\tilde{f}(y))=h(f(y),{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(y)},\emptyset,\iota_{y},\emptyset)=\iota_{y}([\emptyset|\emptyset])=y

and

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(h(x,{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)},\emptyset,\varphi,\emptyset))=\tilde{f}(\varphi([\emptyset|\emptyset]))

\displaystyle=(f(\varphi([\emptyset|\emptyset]),{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)},\emptyset,\iota_{\varphi([\emptyset|\emptyset])},\emptyset)

\displaystyle=(\iota_{x}([\emptyset|\emptyset]),{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)},\emptyset,\varphi,\emptyset)

\displaystyle=(x,{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)},\emptyset,\varphi,\emptyset).

In the above,
f\circ\varphi=\iota_{x}
holds by definition,
and
\varphi=\iota_{\varphi([\emptyset|\emptyset])}
since
ipc-maps from
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}
are determined by their values
on
[\emptyset|\emptyset]
. Thus,
\tilde{f}
defines an
isomorphism between
Y
and its image
Y^{\prime}
. It remains to
show that
Y^{\prime}\subseteq\Cyl(f,g)
is an initial subset.
Suppose
\delta^{1}_{B}(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)\in Y^{\prime}
. Then

({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{1}_{B}})^{-1}(K)={\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U\setminus B}
and

({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{1}_{B}})^{-1}(L)=\emptyset
. Hence,
K
is an initial
subset of
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}
containing an upper face
[\emptyset|B]
, so
also
[\emptyset|\emptyset]\in K
and then
K={\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}
.
Therefore,
L=\emptyset
and

(x,K,L,\varphi,\psi)\in Y^{\prime}
.

Finally, (3)
follows from (2) by reversal, (4)
is obvious from the definition and (5)
follows from (2)–(4) since
{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}\neq\emptyset
for all
x\in X
.

The cylinder
\Cyl(f,\emptyset)
for a map
f:Y\to X
determined by
f(d)=a
.
All edges are labelled by
e
. Cells are marked by sequences
(x,K,\varphi(y))
,
where
y
is the top cell of
K
.

K=0
stands for
\{[e|\emptyset]\}\subseteq\square^{e}
.

Lifting properties of cylinders

The following proposition allows us to use cylinders for replacing
iHDAs with proper iHDAs that accept the same languages.

Proposition 40
.

The projection
p:\Cyl(f,g)\to X
has the future and past lifting
property, as well as the total lifting property with respect to

f(Y)
and
g(Z)
.

Proof 12.5
.

Suppose
x\in X[U]
,
y\in\Cyl(f,g)[U\setminus A]
and

p(y)=\delta_{A}^{0}(x)
. Then
y=(\delta^{0}_{A}(x),K,L,\varphi,\psi)
for
some
K
,
L
,
\varphi
,
\psi
. For

z=(x,{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}}(K),{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}}(L),\varphi\circ({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}})^{-1},\psi\circ({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}})^{-1})
we have

p(z)=x
and
\delta^{0}_{A}(z)=y
. Thus
p
has the FLP, and the PLP
follows by reversal.

For the TLP, let
\alpha=(x_{0},\omega_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in\Path_{X}
,

y,z\in\Cyl(f,g)
, and suppose
p(y)=x_{0}
,
p(z)=x_{n}
. Then

y=(x_{0},K_{0},\emptyset,\varphi_{0},\emptyset)
and

z=(x_{n},\emptyset,L_{n},\emptyset,\psi_{n})
for some
K_{0}
,
\varphi_{0}
,

L_{n}
,
\psi_{n}
, and the remaining items are empty by Lemma

38
. We abbreviate
U_{k}=\Iev(x_{k})
.

Define
K_{k}\subseteq{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U_{k}}
and
\varphi_{k}:K_{k}\to Y
inductively:

•

if
\omega_{k}=d^{1}_{B}
,
B\subseteq U_{k-1}\setminus T_{U_{k-1}}
, then

K_{k}=({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{1}_{B}})^{-1}(K_{k-1})
,

\varphi_{k}=\varphi_{k-1}\circ{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{1}_{B}}
;

•

if
\omega_{k}=d^{0}_{A}
,
A\subseteq U_{k}\setminus S_{U_{k}}
, then

K_{k}={\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}}(K_{k-1})
,

\varphi_{k}=\varphi_{k-1}\circ({\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{d^{0}_{A}})^{-1}
.

We further define
L_{k}
and
\psi_{k}
inductively in backwards
fashion. Now

\beta=((x_{0},K_{0},L_{0},\varphi_{0},\psi_{0}),\omega_{1},\dotsc,(x_{n},K_{n},L_{n},\varphi_{n},\psi_{n}))

is a path in
\Cyl(f,g)
such that
p(\beta)=\alpha
. Moreover,

L_{0}=\emptyset=K_{n}
by Lemma
38
, and
therefore
\mathsf{src}(\beta)=y
and
\mathsf{tgt}(\beta)=z
. The TLP with respect
to
f(Y)
and
g(Z)
is thus established.

Using cylinders we can now show that it suffices to focus on start or
accept proper iHDAs as recognisers of regular languages. Recall that
by Lemma
32
, every regular language is
a finite union of simple regular languages. This prepares us for the gluing
compositions of iHDAs in the following sections.

Proposition 41
.

Every simple regular language is recognised by a start simple and
start proper iHDA, and by an accept simple and accept proper iHDA.

Proof 12.6
.

We prove only the first claim; the second then follows by reversal.
Suppose
L
is recognised by the simple iHDA
X
with start cell

x_{\bot}\in X[U]
. Let
Y
be the iHDA with underlying precubical
set
\Cyl(\iota^{X}_{\bot},\emptyset)
, where
\emptyset:\emptyset\to X

is the empty map. Further, let

y_{\bot}=(x_{\bot},{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U},\emptyset,\id_{{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{U}},\emptyset)
be the
only start cell of
Y
and
Y^{\top}=p^{-1}(X^{\top})
. Since

\iota_{\bot}^{Y}=\widetilde{\iota_{\bot}^{X}}
,
Y
is start proper by
Lemma
39
(b). Moreover, the projection

p:Y\to X
has the FLP by
Proposition
40
. Thus

\Lang(Y)=\Lang(X)=L
by Proposition
37
(a).

Proposition 42
.

If the language
L
is regular, then so is
L\setminus\Id
.

Proof 12.7
.

Suppose first that
L
is simple. Let
X
be a start simple and
start proper iHDA recognising
L
, owing to
Proposition
41
, and let
Y
be the
iHDA with the same underlying ipc-set and start cells as
X
, and
with accept cells
Y^{\top}=X^{\top}\setminus X_{\bot}
. By Lemma

35
, an accepting path
\alpha\in\Path_{X}
is
accepting in
Y
if and only if it has positive length
(
\ev(\alpha)
is not an identity). Thus

\Lang(Y)=\Lang(X)\setminus\Id=L\setminus\Id
is regular. If
L
is
not simple, then let
L=\bigcup_{i}L_{i}
be a finite sum of simple
languages. Then

L\setminus\Id=(\bigcup_{i}L_{i})\setminus\Id=\bigcup_{i}(L_{i}\setminus\Id)
is regular by the first case and Proposition
15
.

13.
Gluing toolbox

We now present several constructions of composite HDAs from simpler
pieces and study properties of their paths.

Sequential gluing

We fix precubical sets
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}
and
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{n-1}
,
initial inclusions
f_{k}:Y_{k}\to X_{k+1}
and final inclusions

g_{k}:Y_{k}\to X_{k}
(
1\leq k<n
). We also assume that the

f_{k-1}(Y_{k-1}),g_{k}(Y_{k})\subseteq X_{k}
are disjoint subsets and
that the
Y_{k}
are acyclic, which means by definition that they are
partially ordered by the reachability preorder
\preceq
(see
Section
5
).

The
sequential gluing
given by this data forms the precubical set

Z=\ZZ_{n}((X_{k})_{k=1}^{n},(Y_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1},(f_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1},(g_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1}),

which is the colimit of the diagram
