}}\hskip-5.69046pt.

(6)

Colimits of presheaves can be calculated pointwise. So
Z
is
obtained from
\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{n}X_{n}
by identifying cells

g_{k}(y)\in X_{k}
and
f_{k}(y)\in X_{k+1}
for all
1\leq k\leq n-1

and
y\in Y_{k}
. Let
j_{k}:X_{k}\to Z
and
i_{k}:Y_{k}\to Z
denote the
structural maps.

Lemma 43
.

The maps
i_{k}
and
j_{k}
are injective for all
1\leq k<n
. Moreover,

i_{k}(Y_{k})=j_{k}(X_{k})\cap j_{k+1}(X_{k+1})
.

Proof 13.1
.

As
f_{k-1}(Y_{k-1})\cap g_{k}(Y_{k})=\emptyset
, each cell

x_{k}\in X_{k}
can be identified with at most one other cell in either

X_{k-1}
or
X_{k+1}
. Consequently, different cells of
X_{k}
are
never identified. Since
j_{k+1}\circ f_{k}=i_{k}=j_{k}\circ g_{k}
, we
have
i_{k}(Y_{k})\subseteq j_{k}(Y_{k})\cap j_{k+1}(Y_{k+1})
. Further, if

j_{k}(x_{k})=j_{k+1}(x_{k+1})
then
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
represent the
same element in
Z
. Equivalence classes have at most two elements,
so
x_{k}=g_{k}(y)
and
x_{k+1}=f_{k}(y)
for some
y\in Y_{k}
.

It follows that
X_{k}
is isomorphic to its image
j_{k}(X_{k})
in
Z
and

Y_{k}
is isomorphic to
i_{k}(Y_{k})
. For simplicity, we regard
X_{k}

and
Y_{k}
as precubical subsets of
Z
such that

Y_{k}=X_{k}\cap X_{k+1}
is a final subset of
X_{k}
and an initial subset
of
X_{k+1}
. We can then turn
Z
into an HDA with
Z_{\bot}=(X_{1})_{\bot}
,

Z^{\top}=(X_{n})^{\top}
for any HDAs
X_{1}
,
X_{n}
.

A
checkpoint sequence
for
an accepting path
\alpha=(z_{1},\varphi_{1},\dotsc,z_{m})\in\Path_{Z}

is a sequence
(r_{0},r_{1},\dotsc,r_{n})
such that

r_{0}=z_{1}
,
r_{n}=z_{m}

and, for every
0<k<n
,
r_{k}\in Y_{k}
and
r_{k}=z_{l}
for some
l=l(k)
.
See Figure
21
for an example.

X_{1}
X_{2}
X_{3}
X_{4}
z_{1}
r_{0}
\bot
z_{2}
z_{3}
r_{1}
z_{4}
r_{2}
z_{5}=z_{8}
z_{6}
z_{7}
z_{9}
z_{10}
r_{3}
z_{11}
z_{12}
r_{4}
\top

Figure 21.
Example of checkpoint sequence.

Lemma 44
.

Every accepting path
\alpha\in\Path_{Z}
admits a checkpoint sequence.
If
(r_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
is a checkpoint sequence for
\alpha
, then

(1)

the indices
l(k)
are uniquely determined by
(r_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
and increasing,

(2)

z_{l(k-1)},z_{l(k-1)+1},\dotsc,z_{l(k)}\in X_{k}
,

(3)

\alpha=\beta_{1}*\dotsc*\beta_{n}
,
where

\beta_{k}=(z_{l(k-1)},\varphi_{l(k-1)},\dotsc,z_{l(k)})\in\Path_{X_{k}}(r_{k-1},r_{k}).

Proof 13.2
.

Define the function
h:Z\to\mathbb{N}
by

h(z)=\begin{cases}2k&\text{if $z\in Y_{k}$,}\\
2k-1&\text{if $z\in X_{k}\setminus(Y_{k-1}\cup Y_{k})$.}\end{cases}

If
z\in Y_{k}
, then
\delta^{0}_{A}(z),\delta^{1}_{B}(z)\in Y_{k}
. If

z\in X_{k}\setminus(Y_{k-1}\cup Y_{k})
, then

\delta^{0}_{A}(z)\in X_{k}\setminus Y_{k}
, since

Y_{k}\subseteq X_{k}
is a final subset, and

\delta^{1}_{B}(z)\in X_{k}\setminus Y_{k-1}
, since

Y_{k-1}\subseteq X_{k}
is an initial subset. Therefore, if

(z,\varphi,z^{\prime})
is a step in
Z
, then either
h(z)=h(z^{\prime})
or

h(z)=h(z^{\prime})-1
.

Let
\alpha=(z_{1},\varphi_{1},\dotsc,z_{m})
. The sequence

h(z_{1}),\dotsc,h(z_{m})
is then increasing by steps of size 0 and 1, and

h(z_{1})\in\{1,2\}
,
h(z_{m})\in\{2n-2,2n-1\}
. Thus, there is
a sequence
l(1)<\dotsm<l(n-1)
of indices such that

h(z_{l(k)})=2k
, that is,
r_{k}=z_{l(k)}\in Y_{k}
and
(r_{k})
is
a checkpoint sequence for
\alpha
.

For (1), fix a checkpoint sequence
(r_{k})
for

\alpha
. Suppose
r_{k}=z_{l}=z_{l^{\prime}}\in Y_{k}
for
l\leq l^{\prime}
.
Then
h(z_{j})=2k
for all
l\leq j\leq l^{\prime}
and

(z_{l},\varphi_{l},\dotsc,z_{l^{\prime}})
is a cycle in
Y_{k}
. By
assumption,
Y_{k}
is acyclic, and therefore
l=l^{\prime}=l(k)
. The
sequence
l(k)
is increasing because the sequence
h(z_{i})

is.

For (2), note that

2k-2=h(z_{l(k-1)})\leq h(z_{j})\leq h(z_{l(k)})=2k
for every

l(k-1)\leq j\leq l(k)
. Thus,
z_{j}\in X_{k}
.

Finally, (3) follows from (2) and injectivity of the
maps
j_{k}
.

Vertical decomposition

We reuse the notation from the previous subsection. We assume that

Y_{k}=\bigsqcup_{q\in C_{k}}Y_{k,q}
is written as a disjoint union
of components. For any sequence
\mathbf{q}=(q_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1}
,

q_{k}\in C_{k}
we write

Z(\mathbf{q})=\ZZ_{n}((X_{k})_{k=1}^{n},(Y_{k,q_{k}})_{k=1}^{n-1},(f_{k}|_{Y_{k,q_{k}}})_{k=1}^{n-1},(g_{k}|_{Y_{k,q_{k}}})_{k=1}^{n-1}).

This is the sequential gluing of the
X_{k}
’s in which we do not glue
along the whole pc-sets
Y_{k}
, but only along their chosen components.
By taking the union indexed by all possible choices we obtain an
HDA that is weakly equivalent to the original sequential gluing:

Proposition 45
.

The map

\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{q}\in C_{1}\times\dotsm\times C_{n-1}}Z(\mathbf{q})\to Z
induced by the identities on
X_{k}
and the
inclusions
Y_{k,q_{k}}\subseteq Y_{k}
is a weak equivalence.

Proof 13.3
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\Path_{Z}
is an accepting path and
(r_{k})
a
checkpoint sequence for
\alpha
. Let
q_{k}
be the index of the
component
Y_{k,q_{k}}
containing
r_{k}
. The
representation
\alpha=\beta_{1}*\dotsm*\beta_{n}

associated to
(r_{k})
also defines a path
\alpha^{\prime}
in

Z(\mathbf{q})
, which obviously maps to
\alpha
under the
canonical map.

Self-gluing

Let
X
be an HDA,
Y
a precubical set,
f:Y\to X
an initial
inclusion and
g:Y\to X
a final inclusion. Suppose
Y
is acyclic
and the sets
f(Y)
,
g(Y)
,
X_{\bot}
and
X^{\top}
are pairwise
disjoint.
Below we identify
f(Y)
, the “initial” copy of
Y
in
X
, with the “final” copy
g(Y)
.
Then we show that the resulting automaton is weakly equivalent to the union of the sequence of the finite gluing compositions.

Define an HDA

V=\VV(X,Y,f,g)=\colim\left(Y\overset{f}{\underset{g}{\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\longrightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}}}X\right).

(7)

Let
j:X\to V
,
i:Y\to V
be the structural maps, let

V_{\bot}=j(X_{\bot})
and
V^{\top}=j(X^{\top})
. Hence
V
is obtained from

X
by identifying cells
f(y)
and
g(y)
for
y\in Y
. Every cell

z\in V
is either represented by a single cell
x\in X
if
z\not\in i(Y)

or by a pair of cells
(f(y),g(y))
,
y\in Y
.

Unlike for sequential gluings, we cannot assume that
X
is proper.
Hence
j
is not necessarily injective and
X
need not be a
precubical subset of
V
.

For
n\geq 1
let

\ZZ_{n}(X,Y,f,g)=\ZZ_{n}((X_{k})_{k=1}^{n},(Y_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1},(f_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1},(g_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1})

where we take
X_{k}=X
,
Y_{k}=Y
,

f_{k}=f
,
g_{k}=g
for all
k
. As before, we let

\ZZ_{n}(X,Y,f,g)_{\bot}=(X_{1})_{\bot},\qquad\ZZ_{n}(X,Y,f,g)^{\top}=(X_{n})^{\top}.

The transformation
from diagram (
6
) to (
7
) that maps
all
X_{k}
’s into
X
,
Y_{k}
’s into
Y
,
f_{k}
’s into
f
and
g_{k}
’s
into
g
induces a map
\pi_{n}:\ZZ_{n}(X,Y,f,g)\to\VV(X,Y,f,g)
.

Proposition 46
.

The map

\pi=\bigsqcup\pi_{n}:\ZZ_{n}(X,Y,f,g)\to\VV(X,Y,f,g)

is a weak equivalence.

Proof 13.4
.

Suppose
\alpha=(z_{1},\varphi_{1},\dotsc,z_{m})\in\Path_{V}
is an
accepting path. We can choose a sequence
1<l(1)<\dotsc<l(n-1)<m

of indices such that
z_{l(k)}\in i(Y)
. Then there exists
b(k)

with
l(k-1)<b(k)<l(k)
such that
z_{b(k)}\not\in i(Y)
(
1<k<n-1
)
and
k
is maximal.
Further, let
b(0)=1
,
b(n)=m
. Equivalently,
we can choose one representative
l(k)
from every sequence of
consecutive cells of
\alpha
that belong to
i(Y)
, and we can
choose representatives
b(k)
from all sequences of cells that not
belong to
i(Y)
. For
1<k<n-1
we choose cells

x^{k}_{s}\in j^{-1}(z_{s})
for
l(k-1)\leq s\leq l(k)
. If

z_{s}\not\in i(Y)
, then
x^{k}_{s}
is the only element of

j^{-1}(z_{s})
. Otherwise,
j^{-1}(z_{s})=\{f(y_{s}),g(y_{s})\}
for some

y_{s}\in Y
and we put
x^{k}_{s}=f(y_{s})
for
s<b(k)
and
x^{k}_{s}=g(y_{s})

for
s>b(k)
.

We show that

\beta_{k}=(x^{k}_{l(k-1)},\varphi_{l(k-1)},\dotsc,x^{k}_{l(k)})
is a
path in
X
. Pick an arbitrary index
s\in\{l(k-1),\dotsc,l(k)-1\}

and assume that
\varphi_{s}=d^{1}_{B}
. We must check that

\delta^{1}_{B}(x^{k}_{s})=x^{k}_{s+1}
. It is clear that both

\delta^{1}_{B}(x^{k}_{s})
and
x^{k}_{s+1}
belong to
j^{-1}(z_{s+1})

because
z_{s+1}=\delta^{1}_{B}(z_{s})
. If
z_{s+1}\not\in i(Y)
, then

j^{-1}(z_{s+1})
has one element and there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise,
j^{-1}(z_{s+1})=\{f(y),g(y)\}
for some
y\in Y
.

•

If
z_{s}\not\in i(Y)
, then
s+1>b(k)
,
and thus, by definition,
x^{k}_{s+1}=g(y)
.
Further,
\delta^{1}_{B}(x^{k}_{s})\not\in f(Y)

(since
x^{k}_{s}\not\in f(Y)
and
f(Y)
is an initial subset of
X
).
Hence
\delta^{1}_{B}(x^{k}_{s})=g(y)=x^{k}_{s+1}
.

•

If
z_{s}\in i(Y)
, then either
s>b(k)
and

x^{k}_{s},x^{k}_{s+1}\in g(Y)
, or
s+1<b(k)
and

x^{k}_{s},x^{k}_{s+1}\in f(Y)
. In both cases

\delta^{1}_{B}(x^{k}_{s})=x^{k}_{s+1}
.

The case
\varphi_{k}=d^{0}_{A}
follows by reversal.
Finally,
\beta_{k}\in\Path_{X}
.

Let
\alpha_{k}=(x^{k}_{l(k-1)},\varphi_{l(k-1)},\dotsc,x^{k}_{l(k)})\in\Path_{X}
. Then
clearly
j(\beta_{k})=\alpha_{k}
and there are cells
y_{k}\in Y
such
that
\mathsf{tgt}(\beta_{k})=g(y_{k})
and
\mathsf{src}(\beta_{k+1})=f(y_{k})
. Then

\gamma=j_{1}(\beta_{1})*\dotsm*j_{n}(\beta_{n}),
where

j_{k}:X\cong X_{k}\to\ZZ_{n}(X,Y,f,g)
is the structural map, is a well-defined
concatenation. This yields

\pi_{n}(\gamma)=\pi_{n}(j_{1}(\beta_{1})*\dotsm*j_{n}(\beta_{n}))=j(\beta_{1})*\dotsm*j(\beta_{n})=\alpha

and the claim holds.

14.
Sequential composition of simple iHDAs

Let
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}\in\text{{{iHDA}}}
. Suppose that
X_{1}
is accept simple and
accept proper, that
X_{2},\dotsc,X_{n-1}
are simple and proper, and
that
X_{n}
is start simple and start proper. Let
x_{\bot}^{k}
(
k>1
)
and
x^{\top}_{k}
, for
k<n
, be the only start and accept cells of

X_{k}
, and assume that
\ev(x^{\top}_{k})=\ev(x_{\bot}^{k+1})=U_{k}
. The
domains of both maps
\Cl(\iota_{\smash[b]{x^{\top}_{k}}})
and
\Cl(\iota_{\smash[b]{x_{\bot}^{k+1}}})
are thus
equal to
\square^{U_{k}}
by Lemma
28
. We do not require
that
\Iev(x^{\top}_{k})=\Iev(x_{\bot}^{k+1})
.

The
sequential composition
of
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}
is the HDA

X_{1}*\dotsm*X_{n}=\ZZ_{n}(\Cl(X_{k})_{k=1}^{n},(\square^{U_{k}})_{k=1}^{n-1},\Cl(\iota_{x_{\bot}^{k}})_{k=1}^{n-1},\Cl(\iota_{x^{\top}_{k}})_{k=1}^{n-1})

(8)

with
(X_{1}*\dotsm*X_{n})_{\bot}=\Cl(X_{1})_{\bot}
,

(X_{1}*\dotsm*X_{n})^{\top}=\Cl(X_{n})^{\top}
. The assumptions required by
(
6
) are thus satisfied: the cubes
\square^{U_{k}}
are acyclic
and the images of
\Cl(\iota_{x_{\bot}^{k}})
and
\Cl(\iota_{x^{\top}_{k}})

are disjoint by properness of the
X_{k}
. Note that the sequential
composition considered is an
n
-ary operation: it maps a sequence of
iHDAs to an HDA. For short, we henceforth write
Z
for the HDA in
(
8
).

We have
\Iev(x^{\top}_{k})={}_{S_{k}}{(U_{k})}_{U_{k}}
and

\Iev(x_{\bot}^{k+1})={}_{U_{k}}{(U_{k})}_{T_{k}}
for some

S_{k},T_{k}\subseteq U_{k}
, which are not necessarily disjoint. The

i_{k}:\square^{U_{k}}\to Z
,
j_{k}:\Cl(X_{k})\to Z
are structural maps, which
we sometimes omit, regarding
\Cl(X_{k})
and
\square^{U_{k}}
as precubical
subsets of
Z
. Let

u_{k}=i_{k}([\emptyset|\emptyset])=j_{k}([x_{k}^{\top};\emptyset,\emptyset])=j_{k+1}([x^{k+1}_{\bot};\emptyset,\emptyset])
.

Proposition 47
.

\Lang(X_{1})*\dotsm*\Lang(X_{n})\subseteq\Lang(X_{1}*\dotsm*X_{n})
.

Proof 14.1
.

If
P\in\Lang(X_{1})*\dotsm*\Lang(X_{n})
, then

P\sqsubseteq Q_{1}*\dotsm*Q_{n}
for some
Q_{k}\in\Lang(X_{k})
. Choose
accepting paths
\beta_{k}\in\Path_{\Cl(X_{k})}
such that

\ev(\beta_{k})=Q_{k}
, according to
Proposition
29
. Since

\mathsf{tgt}(\beta_{k})=[x_{k}^{\top};\emptyset,\emptyset]
and

\mathsf{src}(\beta_{k+1})=[x^{k+1}_{\bot};\emptyset,\emptyset]
represent the
same cell
u_{k}
in
Z
, the
\beta_{k}
can be composed. Then

P\sqsubseteq Q_{1}*\dotsm*Q_{n}=\ev(\beta_{1})*\dotsm*\ev(\beta_{n})=\ev(\beta_{1}*\dotsc*\beta_{n})\in\Lang(X_{1}*\dotsm*X_{n}).

The
defect
of an accepting path
\alpha\in\Path_{Z}
is

\min\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(|U_{k}|-\dim(r_{k}))\mid\text{$(r_{k})_{k=0}^{n}$ is a checkpoint sequence for $\alpha$}\right\}.

The defect of
\alpha
measures how far
\alpha
passes from the cells
u_{k}
.
It is non-negative since

\dim(\square^{U_{k}})=|U_{k}|
and equal to
0
if and only if
r_{k}=u_{k}

for all
1\leq k<n

since
u_{k}
are the only cells in
\square^{U}
of dimension
|U_{k}|
.
A checkpoint sequence for which the minimum is obtained is called

maximal
, because it consists of cells with the maximal dimensions
possible.

Lemma 48
.

Let
\alpha\in\Path_{Z}
be a path that is not subsumed by another
path with a smaller defect. Then
(u_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
is a checkpoint sequence
for
\alpha
(we assume
u_{0}=\mathsf{src}(\alpha)
,
u_{k}=\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha)
).

Proof 14.2
.

Let
(r_{k})
be a maximal checkpoint sequence for
\alpha
. By
assumption,
r_{k}=\delta_{E_{k},F_{k}}(u_{k})
and
E_{k},F_{k}
are uniquely
determined by injectivity of
i_{k}
. We wish to show that

E_{k}=F_{k}=\emptyset
.

Let
r_{0}=\mathsf{src}(\alpha)
,
r_{n}=\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha)
. Let

\alpha=\beta_{1}*\dotsm*\beta_{n}
,

\beta_{k}\in\Path_{X_{k}}(r_{k-1},r_{k})
, be the representation
determined by
(r_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
according to
Lemma
44
.(c). By Lemma
33
, every
path
\beta_{k}
is subsumed by a path
\gamma_{k}
of the form

(r_{k-1}=[y;C_{k}\cup A_{k},D_{k}]\mathrel{\nearrow^{A_{k}}}[y;C_{k},D_{k}])*\beta^{\prime}_{k}*([z;C_{k},D_{k}]\mathrel{\searrow_{B_{k}}}[z;C_{k},D_{k}\cup B_{k}]=r_{k}).

We show that
B_{k}=\emptyset
. For
k=n
,

[z;C_{k},D_{k}\cup B_{k}]=[x_{n}^{\top};\emptyset,\emptyset]\in\Cl(X_{n})^{\top}
and thus
C_{n}=D_{n}\cup B_{n}=\emptyset
. For
k<n
, the
cell
r_{k}
is a face of
u_{k}
. Then
[z;C_{k},D_{k}\cup B_{k}]
is a face
of
[x^{\top}_{k};\emptyset,\emptyset]
by injectivity of
j_{k}
,
z
is
a face of
x^{\top}_{k}
by Lemma
31
and

[z;C_{k},D_{k}]
is a face of
[x^{\top}_{k};\emptyset,\emptyset]
by the
same lemma. Thus
[z;C_{k},D_{k}]
is a face of
u_{k}
, or equivalently,

[z;C_{k},D_{k}]\in\square^{U_{k}}
. If
B\neq\emptyset
, then

[z;C_{k},D_{k}]
is of higher dimension than
r_{k}=[z;C_{k},D_{k}\cup B_{k}]
.
Then,

(r_{1},\dotsc,r_{k-1},[z;C_{k},D_{k}],r_{k+1},\dotsc,r_{n-1})

is a checkpoint sequence that contradicts the maximality of
(r_{k})

and shows that
B_{k}=\emptyset
. Similarly,
A_{k}=\emptyset
follows
by reversal. This shows that every
\beta_{k}
has the form

([z^{k}_{j};C_{k},D_{k}],\varphi^{k}_{j})_{j=1}^{m_{k}}
, that is, the second and
third coordinates in
\beta_{k}
remain constant.

Next, we relate the sets
C_{k}
,
D_{k}
,
E_{k}
and
F_{k}
. For every

k=1,\dotsc,n-1
,

r_{k}=\delta_{E_{k},F_{k}}([x^{\top}_{k};\emptyset,\emptyset])=[\delta^{0}_{E_{k}\setminus S_{k}}(x^{\top}_{k});E_{k}\cap S_{k},F_{k}],

and hence
C_{k}=E_{k}\cap S_{k}
,
D_{k}=F_{k}
. Moreover,

r_{k}=\delta_{E_{k},F_{k}}([x_{\bot}^{k+1};\emptyset,\emptyset])=[\delta^{0}_{F_{k}\setminus T_{k}}(x_{\bot}^{k+1});E_{k},F_{k}\cap T_{k}],

and thus
C_{k+1}=E_{k}
,
D_{k+1}=F_{k}\cap T_{k}
. Therefore,

C_{k}\subseteq C_{k+1}
and
D_{k}\supseteq D_{k+1}
. Furthermore,

C_{1}=D_{1}=\emptyset
since
\mathsf{src}(\beta_{1})\in\Cl(X_{1})_{\bot}
and

C_{n}=D_{n}=\emptyset
since
\mathsf{tgt}(\beta_{n})\in\Cl(X_{n})^{\top}
. The sets

C_{k}
,
D_{k}
,
E_{k}
and
F_{k}
are therefore empty,

r_{k}=\delta_{E_{k},F_{k}}(u_{k})=u_{k}
and
\alpha
has defect
0
.

Lemma 49
.

Every accepting path
\gamma\in\Path_{Z}
is subsumed by a path

\alpha
with checkpoint sequence
(\mathsf{src}(\alpha),u_{1},\dots,u_{n-1},\mathsf{tgt}(\alpha))
. Thus

\alpha
traverses the interiors of the gluing cubes
\square^{U_{k}}
.

Proof 14.3
.

Let
\alpha\in\Path_{Z}
be a path with minimal defect among all
paths subsuming
\gamma
. The conclusion then follows by
Proposition
48
.

Proposition 50
.

\Lang(X_{1}*\dotsm*X_{n})=\Lang(X_{1})*\dots*\Lang(X_{n})
.

Proof 14.4
.

Let
P\in\Lang(Z)
and
\gamma\in\Path_{Z}
be an accepting path
such that
\ev(\gamma)=P
. Then
\gamma
is subsumed by an

\alpha\in\Path_{Z}
with checkpoint sequence
(u_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
by Lemma
49
and the
\beta_{k}\in\Path_{\Cl(X_{k})}

determined by
(u_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
, according to
Lemma
44
, are accepting. Therefore

\displaystyle P=\ev(\gamma)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\ev(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\ev(j_{1}(\beta_{1})*\dotsm*j_{n}(\beta_{n}))

\displaystyle=\ev(\beta_{1})*\dotsm*\ev(\beta_{n})\in\Lang(\Cl(X_{1}))*\dots*\Lang(\Cl(X_{n}))

\displaystyle=\Lang(X_{1})*\dots*\Lang(X_{n})

and thus
\Lang(Z)\subseteq\Lang(X_{1})*\dots*\Lang(X_{n})
.
The converse inclusion follows from Proposition
47
.

After all these preparations we can finally show that gluing
compositions of regular languages are regular.

Proof 14.5

(Proof of Proposition
19
)
.

Let
L
and
M
be regular languages. If they are simple, then
there exist simple iHDAs
X
and
Y
that recognise
L
and
M
.
Proposition
41
allows us to assume
that
X
is accept simple and accept proper and that
Y
is start
simple and start proper. Proposition
50
then
implies that

\Lang(X*Y)=\Lang(X)*\Lang(Y)=L*M

is regular. Otherwise, if
L
and
M
are not simple, then

L=\bigcup_{i}L_{i}
and
M=\bigcup_{j}M_{j}
for simple regular languages

L_{i}
and
M_{j}
by Lemma
32
. In this case,

L*M=\big(\bigcup_{i}L_{i}\big)*\big(\bigcup_{j}M_{j}\big)=\bigcup_{i}\bigcup_{j}L_{i}*M_{j}

is regular by Proposition
15
.

15.
Kleene plus

Suppose
X
is an iHDA such that
\Lang(X)
is separated. We
construct an HDA
X^{+}
such that
\Lang(X^{+})=\Lang(X)^{+}
. We wish to
identify accept cells with start cells that have the same events. But
identifying all of them would produce too many accepting paths. For
every accept cell
y
we thus add a copy for every start cell
compatible with
y
, and an extra one to replace the original one –
and likewise for start cells. We must then ensure that the iHDA
constructed is proper. The following fact ensures that we can
construct the cylinder below.

Lemma 51
.

Let
X\in\text{{{iHDA}}}
and
\Lang(X)
be separated. Then

\im(\iota^{X}_{\bot})\cap\im(\iota_{X}^{\top})=\emptyset
.

Proof 15.1
.

Suppose
y\in\im(\iota^{X}_{\bot})\cap\im(\iota_{X}^{\top})
for some

y\in X[V]
. Then
\delta^{1}_{B}(x)=y=\delta^{0}_{A}(z)
for some

U,W\in{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square
,
x\in X_{\bot}[U]
,
z\in X^{\top}[W]
,
B\subseteq U
,

A\subseteq W
and
U\setminus B=V=W\setminus A
because
start/accept cells have only upper/lower faces. Then every event of
the ipomset

\ev\left(x\mathrel{\searrow_{B}}y\mathrel{\nearrow^{A}}z\right)={}_{U}{U}_{V}*{}_{V}{W}_{W}

in
\Lang(X)
lies in one of the interfaces
U
,
W
and

\Lang(X)
is not separated.

Let
G=\{(y,x)\mid y\in X^{\top},\;x\in X_{\bot},\;\ev(x)=\ev(y)\}
, let

G_{\bot}=\{(\bot,x)\mid x\in X_{\bot}\}
and

G^{\top}=\{(y,\top)\mid y\in X^{\top}\}
. Define

J_{\bot}=\bigsqcup_{(y,x)\in G\cup G_{\bot}}{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(x)}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad J^{\top}=\bigsqcup_{(y,x)\in G\cup G^{\top}}{\textup{I}}\mkern-1.0mu\square^{\Iev(y)}.

Further, let
w_{\bot}=\bigsqcup\iota_{x}:J_{\bot}\to X
,

w^{\top}=\bigsqcup\iota_{y}:J^{\top}\to X
. The maps
w_{\bot}
and

w^{\top}
are similar to the start and accept maps, but more than one
cube can be mapped into a start or accept cell.

We define the
spider
of
X
as the cylinder

\Spider(X)=\Cyl(w_{\bot},w^{\top})
. It is well-defined because

\im(w_{\bot})\cap\im(w^{\top})=\im(\iota^{X}_{\bot})\cap\im(\iota_{X}^{\top})=\emptyset
, since
\Lang(X)
is separated and by Lemma
51
. The spider is equipped with an initial
inclusion
\widetilde{w}_{\bot}:J_{\bot}\to\Spider(X)
and a final
inclusion
\widetilde{w}^{\top}:J^{\top}\to\Spider(X)
(Lemma
39
). We write

\displaystyle[{y}{\backslash}{x}]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{w}_{\bot}((y,x),[\emptyset|\emptyset])\in\Spider(X)[\Iev(x)],

\displaystyle[{y}{/}{x}]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{w}^{\top}((y,x),[\emptyset|\emptyset])\in\Spider(X)[\Iev(y)]

for
(y,x)\in G\cup G_{\bot}
in the upper line and
(y,x)\in G\cup G^{\top}
in the lower line.

Each cell
[{y}{\backslash}{x}]
is a “copy” of
x
that is to be connected to a
copy
[{y}{/}{x}]
of
y
. Such copies serve as start and accept cells
of
\Spider(X)
: we put

\Spider(X)_{\bot}=\{[{\bot}{\backslash}{x}]\mid x\in X_{\bot}\}
and

\Spider(X)^{\top}=\{[{y}{/}{\top}]\mid y\in X^{\top}\}
. See Figure

22
for an example.

We also use
\Spider(X)
in combination with other sets of start and
accept cells: for each subset
S,T\subseteq\Spider(X)
,

\Spider(X)_{S}^{T}
denotes the iHDA with underlying ipc-set

\Spider(X)
, start cells
S
and accept cells
T
. We use a similar
convention for
X
.

[{\bot}{\backslash}{x}]
[{\bot}{\backslash}{w}]
[{y}{/}{\top}]
[{z}{/}{\top}]
[{y}{\backslash}{x}]
[{z}{\backslash}{x}]
[{y}{/}{w}]
[{y}{/}{x}]
[{y}{\backslash}{w}]
[{z}{\backslash}{w}]
[{z}{/}{w}]
[{z}{/}{x}]

Figure 22.
A specimen of spider

Lemma 52
.

For
(y^{\prime},x)\in G\cup G_{\bot}
,

(y,x^{\prime})\in G\cup G^{\top}
,
the projection map

p:\Spider(X)_{[{y^{\prime}}{\backslash}{x}]}^{[{y}{/}{x^{\prime}}]}\to X_{x}^{y}

is a weak equivalence.

Proof 15.2
.

We have
p([{y^{\prime}}{\backslash}{x}])=x\in\im(w_{\bot})
and

p([{y}{/}{x^{\prime}}])=y\in\im(w^{\top})
. Since
p
has the TLP with
respect to
x
,
y
by Proposition
40
,
it is a weak equivalence by Proposition
37
(c).

Every accepting path
\alpha\in\Path_{X}(x,y)
can therefore be lifted
to a path from any copy of
x
to any copy of
y
.

Next, we identify “start copies” with “accept copies”. To achieve
this we need to pass to HDAs. We abbreviate

\CSpider(X)=\Cl(\Spider(X))
. We regard cells
[{y}{\backslash}{x}]
and

[{y}{/}{x}]
as cells of
\CSpider(X)
– formally, we should write

[[{y}{\backslash}{x}];\emptyset,\emptyset]
and
[[{y}{/}{x}];\emptyset,\emptyset]

instead.

Let
\Weld_{\bot}(X)\subseteq J_{\bot}
,
\Weld^{\top}(X)\subseteq J^{\top}

be the unions of cubes indexed by
G
(we omit
G_{\bot}
and
G^{\top}
, respectively).
The closures of
\Weld_{\bot}(X)
and
\Weld^{\top}(X)
are naturally isomorphic to

\Weld(X)=\bigsqcup_{(x,y)\in G}\square^{\ev(x)}=\bigsqcup_{(x,y)\in G}\square^{\ev(y)}

by Lemma
28
. Let
f,g:\Weld(X)\to\CSpider(X)
be
the closures of the restrictions

f=\Cl(\widetilde{w}_{\bot}|_{\Weld_{\bot}(X)})
and

g=\Cl(\widetilde{w}^{\top}|_{\Weld^{\top}(X)})
. Since

\Weld_{\bot}(X)\subseteq J_{\bot}
and
\Weld^{\top}(X)\subseteq J^{\top}

are initial and final subsets,
f
is an initial inclusion and
g
a
final one by Lemma
34
. Now we are ready
to define the HDA

X^{+}=\VV(\CSpider(X),\Weld(X),f,g).

It remains to show that
\Lang(X^{+})=\Lang(X)^{+}
. The map

\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\ZZ_{n}(\CSpider(X),\Weld(X),f,g)\to\VV(\CSpider(X),\Weld(X),f,g)

(9)

is a weak equivalence by Proposition
46
.
For any sequence
\Gamma=(x_{k},y_{k})_{k=1}^{n-1}\in G^{n-1}
and

U_{k}=\ev(x_{k})=\ev(y_{k})
we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{n}(X;\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\ZZ_{n}(\CSpider(X)_{k=1}^{n},(\square^{U_{k}})_{k=1}^{n-1},\Cl(\iota_{[{y_{k}}{\backslash}{x_{k}}]})_{k=1}^{n-1},\Cl(\iota_{[{y_{k}}{/}{x_{k}}]})_{k=1}^{n-1})

\displaystyle=\Spider(X)_{[\bot\backslash X_{\bot}]}^{[y_{1}/x_{1}]}*\Spider(X)_{[y_{1}\backslash x_{1}]}^{[y_{2}/x_{2}]}*\dotsm*\Spider(X)_{[y_{n-1}\backslash x_{n-1}]}^{[X^{\top}/\top]},

where

[\bot\backslash X_{\bot}]=\{[\bot\backslash x]\mid x\in X_{\bot}\}
,

[X^{\top}/\top]=\{[y/\top]\mid y\in X^{\top}\}
.

Proposition
45
,
applied to the decomposition (
9
),
shows that

\bigsqcup_{\Gamma\in G^{n-1}}\mathfrak{S}_{n}(X;\Gamma)\to\ZZ_{n}(\CSpider(X),\Weld(X),f,g)

is a weak equivalence. As weakly equivalent HDAs have equal languages
(Lemma
13
), we obtain the following fact.

Lemma 53
.

\Lang(X^{+})=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\bigcup_{\Gamma\in G^{n-1}}\Lang(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(X;\Gamma))
.

Proposition
50
and Lemma
52
then imply
that

\displaystyle\Lang(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(X;\Gamma))\hskip-50.00008pt

\displaystyle=\Lang\left(\Spider(X)_{[{\bot}{\backslash}{X_{\bot}}]}^{[y_{1}/x_{1}]}\right)*\Lang\left(\Spider(X)_{[y_{1}\backslash x_{1}]}^{[y_{2}/x_{2}]}\right)*\dotsm*\Lang\left(\Spider(X)_{[y_{n-1}\backslash x_{n-1}]}^{[{X^{\top}}/\top]}\right)

\displaystyle=\Lang(X_{X_{\bot}}^{y_{1}})*\Lang(X_{x_{1}}^{y_{2}})*\dotsm*\Lang(X_{x_{n-1}}^{X^{\top}}).

(10)

Lemma 54
.

\bigcup_{\Gamma\in G^{n-1}}\Lang(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(X;\Gamma))=\Lang(X)^{n}
.

Proof 15.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\Lang(X)^{n}

\displaystyle=\Lang(X)*\dotsm*\Lang(X)

\displaystyle=\Big({\bigcup_{y_{1}\in X^{\top}}}\Lang(X_{X_{\bot}}^{y_{1}})\Big)*\Big(\underset{y_{2}\in X^{\top}}{\bigcup_{x_{1}\in X_{\bot}}}\Lang(X_{x_{1}}^{y_{2}})\Big)*\dotsm*\Big({\bigcup_{x_{n-1}\in X_{\bot}}}\Lang(X_{x_{n-1}}^{X^{\top}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\underset{y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n-1}\in X^{\top}}{\bigcup_{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n-1}\in X_{\bot}}}\Lang(X_{X_{\bot}}^{y_{1}})*\Lang(X_{x_{1}}^{y_{2}})*\dotsm*\Lang(X_{x_{n-1}}^{X^{\top}})

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\bigcup_{\Gamma=(x_{k},y_{k})\in G^{n-1}}\Lang(X_{X_{\bot}}^{y_{1}})*\Lang(X_{x_{1}}^{y_{2}})*\dotsm*\Lang(X_{x_{n-1}}^{X^{\top}})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{e:LanguageOfSnakes}}{=}\bigcup_{\Gamma\in G^{n-1}}\Lang(\mathfrak{S}_{n}(X;\Gamma)).

In
(\dagger)
we use the fact that

\Lang(X_{x_{k-1}}^{y_{k}})*\Lang(X_{x_{k}}^{y_{k+1}})=\emptyset

whenever
\ev(y_{k})\neq\ev(x_{k})
.

Proposition 55
.

The Kleene plus of a separated regular language is regular.

Proof 15.4
.

From Lemmas
53
and
54
it follows that

\Lang(X^{+})=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\Lang(X)^{n}=\Lang(X)^{+}.

Finally we can prove that the Kleene plus of any regular
language is regular.

Proof 15.5

(Proof of Proposition
20
)
.

Suppose
L
is regular. If
L\cap\Id=\emptyset
, then
L^{n}
is
separated for sufficiently large
n
by Lemma
6
. In this case,

L^{+}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}L^{i}\cup\Big(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}L^{i}\Big)*(L^{n})^{+}

is regular by Propositions
15
,

19
and
55
.
If
L\cap\Id\neq\emptyset
, then

L^{+}=((L\cap\Id)\cup(L\setminus\Id))^{+}=(L\cap\Id)\cup(L\setminus\Id)^{+}

is regular by
Propositions
15
and
42
.

As outlined in Section
6
, the proof of the Kleene
theorem for higher-dimensional automata is now complete.

16.
Conclusion

Automata accept languages, but higher-dimensional automata have for a
long time been an exception to this rule. Here, we have proved a
Kleene theorem for HDAs, connecting models to behaviours through an
equivalence between regular and rational languages.

Showing that regular languages are rational was quite direct, while the
converse direction required some effort. One reason is that HDAs may
be glued not only at states, but also at higher-dimensional cells.
This led us to consider languages of ipomsets and to equip HDAs with
interfaces, yielding iHDAs. After showing that HDAs and iHDAs
recognise the same languages, we used constructions inspired by
topology to glue (i)HDAs and show that rational operations on
languages can be reflected by operations on them.

Kleene theorems build bridges between machines and languages, and
there is a vast literature on this subject. In non-interleaving
concurrency, one school considers Mazurkiewicz trace
languages. Zielonka introduces asynchronous automata and shows that
languages are regular if and only if they are recognisable

[
Zie87
]
. Droste’s automata with
concurrency relations have similar properties

[
Dro94
]
. Yet not all rational trace languages
that are generated from singletons using union, concatenation and
Kleene star are recognisable
[
CG95
]
.
Trace languages use a binary notion of independence and already
2-dimensional HDAs may exhibit behaviour that cannot be captured by
trace languages
[
Gou02
]
.

Another school studies Kleene theorems for series-parallel pomset
languages and automata models for these, such as branching and pomset
automata
[
LW00
,
KBL+19
]
, and Petri automata

[
BP17
]
. Series-parallel pomsets are
incomparable to the interval orders accepted by Petri nets or
HDAs
[
Vog92
,
FJSZ22b
]
.

HDAs have been developed first of all with a view on operational,
topological and geometric aspects of concurrency, see

[
FGH+16
]
and the extensive bibliography in

[
vG06a
]
. But languages for HDAs have only
been introduced recently
[
FJSZ21
]
. Topological intuitions have
also guided our present work, for example in the cylinder
construction.

Our formalisation of (i)HDAs as presheaves on a category of labelled
posets opens up connections to presheaf automata

[
Sob15
]
, coalgebra, and open maps

[
JNW96
]
, which we intend to explore.
Finally, our introduction of iHDA morphisms akin to cofibrations and
trivial fibrations hints at factorisation systems. Weak factorisation
systems and model categories have been considered in a bisimulation
context, for example in
[
KR05
]
, and we wonder about the
connection.

Appendix A
Definitions of HDAs

Precubical sets and HDAs appear in different incarnations in the
literature, all of them more or less equivalent. We discuss some of
these in this Appendix to relate them with our own approach, but make
no claim as to completeness.

Precubical sets

Precubical sets à la Grandis
[
GM03
,
Gra09
]
are presheaves on a small category
\square_{\textup{G}}
, defined
by the following data:

•

objects are
\{0,1\}^{n}
for
n\geq 0
;

•

elementary coface maps
d_{i}^{\nu}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n+1}
,
for
i=1,\dotsc,n+1
and
\nu=0,1
, are given by

d_{i}^{\nu}(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{i-1},\nu,t_{i},\dotsc,t_{n})
.

Elementary coface maps compose to coface maps

\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
for
n\geq m
in the standard way.

\square_{\textup{G}}
-sets, that is, elements
X\in\text{{{Set}}}^{\square_{\textup{G}}^{\textup{op}}}
, are then
graded sets
X=\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, where
X_{n}=X[\{0,1\}^{n}]
, together
with face maps
X_{n}\to X_{m}
for
n\geq m
. The elementary face maps
are denoted
\delta_{i}^{\nu}=X[d_{i}^{\nu}]
. They must satisfy the

precubical identity
, for any
\nu,\mu\in\{0,1\}
and
i<j
:

\delta_{i}^{\nu}\delta_{j}^{\mu}=\delta_{j-1}^{\mu}\delta_{i}^{\nu}.

(11)

This description of
\square_{\textup{G}}
-sets may be taken as a definition
without using presheaves. For example, van Glabbeek

[
vG06a
]
defines a precubical set

Q=(Q,s,t)
as a family of sets
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and maps

s_{i}:Q_{n}\to Q_{n-1}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that

\alpha_{i}\circ\beta_{j}=\beta_{j-1}\circ\alpha_{i}
for all

1\leq i<j\leq n
and
\alpha,\beta\in\{s,t\}
. This is equivalent to
the above.

We have previously introduced another base category,
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
,
defined as follows
[
FJSZ21
]
:

•

objects are totally ordered sets
(S,{\dashrightarrow_{S}})
;

•

morphisms
S\to T
are pairs
(f,\varepsilon)
, where

f:S\hookrightarrow T
is an order preserving injection and

\varepsilon:T\to\{0,\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox to7.29pt{\vbox to4.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.77 0 L 4.77 5.96 L 9.53 5.96}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}},1\}
satisfies

f(S)=\varepsilon^{-1}(\raisebox{1.0pt}{\hbox to7.29pt{\vbox to4.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 4.77 0 L 4.77 5.96 L 9.53 5.96}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})
.

The element
stands for “active”, a notation previously used
by van Glabbeek. Writing
A=\varepsilon^{-1}(0)
and
B=\varepsilon^{-1}(1)

makes the above notion of morphism equivalent to the triples

(f,A,B)
consisting of
f:S\hookrightarrow T
(order preserving and
injective) and
A,B\subseteq T
such that
T=A\sqcup f(S)\sqcup B

(disjoint union). Except for the labels, this is our definition of

in Section
3
.

Using this definition, it can be shown that the full subcategory of

\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
spanned by the objects
\emptyset
and
\{1,\dotsc,n\}

for
n\geq 1
is skeletal and equivalent to
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}

[
FJSZ21
]
.
Moreover, this subcategory,
\square_{\textup{Z}}
, is isomorphic to
\square_{\textup{G}}
, and the
presheaf categories on
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
and on
\square_{\textup{Z}}
(and thus also on

\square_{\textup{G}}
) are uniquely naturally isomorphic. It is clear that
\square_{\textup{Z}}

is a representative of the quotient of
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
with respect to
isomorphisms, so, except for the labelling, this is again our category

\square
from Section
3
.

The advantage of
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
and
over the skeletal versions
is that the precubical identity (
11
) is automatic and that
there is a built-in notion of events and actions, that is, in a

\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
-set
X
, each cell
x\in X[U]
has events
U
.

HDAs

Higher-dimensional automata are
\Sigma
-labelled precubical sets with
specified start and accept cells. The labelling may be obtained using
the labelling object
\mathord{!}\Sigma

[
Gou02
]
. This is
the precubical set with
\mathord{!}\Sigma_{n}=\Sigma^{n}
and

\delta_{i}^{\nu}((a_{1},\dotsc,a_{n}))=(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{i-1},a_{i+1},\dotsc,a_{n})
. A labelled precubical set is then a precubical map

X\to\mathord{!}\Sigma
, that is, an object of the slice category of
precubical sets over
\mathord{!}\Sigma
.

Each labelling function
\lambda:X\to\mathord{!}\Sigma
induces a function

\lambda_{1}:X_{1}\to\Sigma
satisfying

\lambda_{1}(\delta_{1}^{0}(x))=\lambda_{1}(\delta_{1}^{1}(x))
and

\lambda_{1}(\delta_{2}^{0}(x))=\lambda_{1}(\delta_{2}^{1}(x))
for all

x\in X_{2}
. Conversely, each such function extends uniquely to a
precubical map
X\to\mathord{!}\Sigma

[
FJSZ21
, Lemma 14]
, so that

\lambda_{1}
may be taken as the primary definition instead. This is
the approach in
[
vG06a
]
, where HDAs are
defined as precubical sets
Q
equipped with functions

\lambda_{1}\to\Sigma
such that
\lambda_{1}(s_{i}(q))=\lambda_{1}(t_{i}(q))

for all
q\in Q_{2}
and
i=1,2
, and subsets of start and accept states

I,F\subseteq Q_{0}
.

Regarded as a presheaf,
\mathord{!}\Sigma(S)=\text{{{Set}}}(S,\Sigma)
. Hence

\mathord{!}\Sigma
is representable in
Set
via the forgetful functor

\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}\to\text{{{Set}}}

[
FJSZ21
]
. Labels can thus be integrated into
the base category, which turns
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
into our category
,
with labelled totally ordered sets as objects. Using

instead of
\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\textup{Z}}
allows working in a labelled setting ab
initio instead of taking a slice category.

To summarise, starting from an HDA
X
as defined in this article, an
HDA
(Q,s,t,\lambda_{1},I,F)
à la van Glabbeek

[
vG06a
]
can be obtained as follows:

•

Q_{n}=\bigsqcup_{U\in\square,\;|U|=n}X[U]
.

•

If
x\in X[U]
, then
s_{i}(x)=\delta^{0}_{u}(x)
and
t_{i}(x)=\delta^{1}_{u}(x)
,
where
u\in U
is the
i
-th smallest element of
U
in the order

\dashrightarrow_{U}
.

•

If
x\in X[U]\subseteq Q_{1}
with

U=(\{e\},\emptyset,\lambda(e)=a)
, then
\lambda_{1}(x)=a
.

•

I=X_{\bot}
,
F=X^{\top}
.

Conversely, let
(Q,s,t,\lambda_{1},I,F)
be an HDA à la van Glabbeek.
Then there are unique labelling functions
\lambda_{n}:Q_{n}\to\Sigma^{n}

that satisfy
\lambda_{n-1}(\alpha_{i}(q))=\delta_{i}(\lambda_{n}(q))

[
FJSZ21
, Lemma 14]
, where
\alpha\in\{s,t\}
and
\delta_{i}

discards the
i
-th element of a sequence. We can then construct an HDA
X
in
the sense of this article as follows:

•

X[U]=\{q\in Q_{n}\mid\lambda_{n}(q)=U\}
for
U\in\hbox{\hbox to7.5pt{\vbox to7.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{\ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 8.99 L 8.99 8.99 L 8.99 0 Z M 8.99 8.99}{fill:none} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and

|U|=n
.

•

\delta^{0}_{a}(q)=s_{i}(q)
and
\delta^{1}_{a}(q)=t_{i}(q)
for

q\in X[U]
and
a\in U
the
i
-th smallest element of
U
in the
order
\dashrightarrow_{U}
. The remaining face maps are composites of these.

•

X_{\bot}=I
and
X^{\top}=F
.
